## [RadarGen: Diffusion Model Generates Radar Point Clouds from Cameras](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.17897) ⭐️ 8.0/10

RadarGen introduces a diffusion model that synthesizes realistic automotive radar point clouds from multi-view camera images, using bird's-eye-view representations and cues from pretrained foundation models. The method is evaluated on large-scale driving data and shows improved alignment with real radar distributions. This work addresses a critical gap in multimodal sensor simulation for autonomous driving, enabling scalable generation of radar data from widely available camera datasets. It could reduce the need for expensive real-world radar collection and improve the training of perception models across modalities. RadarGen adapts efficient image-latent diffusion to the radar domain by encoding radar measurements in bird's-eye-view form that includes spatial structure, radar cross section (RCS), and Doppler attributes. A lightweight recovery step reconstructs point clouds from generated maps, and the method incorporates BEV-aligned depth, semantic, and motion cues from foundation models to guide generation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Bird's-eye-view (BEV) representation provides an ego-vehicle-centered, 360-degree top-down view of the surroundings, which is crucial for perception and decision-making in autonomous driving. Latent diffusion models, such as Stable Diffusion, generate images by learning to denoise compressed latent representations, which is more efficient than pixel-space diffusion. Radar sensors provide complementary information to cameras, such as velocity and reflectivity, but collecting large-scale radar data is costly, motivating generative approaches.

## [SOOPER: Safe RL Exploration via Conservative Policy Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.19612) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces SOOPER, a model-based reinforcement learning algorithm that uses conservative policy priors and probabilistic dynamics models to guarantee safety during exploration while achieving optimality. It provides theoretical guarantees and validates them on benchmarks and real-world hardware. Safe exploration is critical for deploying RL in real-world environments where unsafe actions can cause damage. SOOPER offers a principled approach that balances exploration and safety, potentially enabling broader adoption of RL in robotics and other safety-critical domains. SOOPER optimistically explores using probabilistic dynamics models but pessimistically falls back to the conservative policy prior when needed. The paper proves safety guarantees throughout learning and bounds cumulative regret, with experiments on safe RL benchmarks and hardware showing scalability and state-of-the-art performance.

**Background**: Reinforcement learning (RL) agents typically learn by trial and error, which can be unsafe in real-world settings. Safe exploration aims to minimize risky actions while still enabling learning. MOSAIC is a new large-scale benchmark that jointly assesses the moral, social, and individual characteristics of LLMs using nine validated questionnaires and four platform-based games, totaling over 600 curated questions and scenarios. It is the first benchmark of its kind to go beyond Moral Foundation Theory (MFT) alone. This benchmark addresses a significant gap in LLM evaluation by incorporating social and individual dimensions, which are crucial for understanding ethical reasoning in real-world applications like healthcare and decision-making. It provides empirical evidence that MFT alone is insufficient, potentially influencing how AI ethics are assessed and guiding future model development. The benchmark includes nine validated questionnaires from moral philosophy, psychology, and social theory, along with four games probing morally ambiguous scenarios. It was validated across three models from different families, and the dataset and Python library are publicly released.

MOSAIC aims to provide a more holistic evaluation by incorporating these additional dimensions.

## [In-Context Superposition: LLMs Show Human-Like Working Memory Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09670) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper proposes that working memory limitations in large language models (LLMs) arise from a general trade-off of shared representations, termed 'in-context superposition.' The authors show that while a two-layer transformer can solve a working memory task perfectly, diverse pretrained LLMs exhibit human-like performance declines with memory load and recency biases. This work bridges cognitive science and AI by suggesting that working memory constraints are not unique to biological brains but emerge from fundamental representational trade-offs. It could inform future LLM architectures that mitigate interference, potentially improving their reasoning and multi-step task performance. The paper demonstrates that LLMs encode multiple memories in entangled representations, progressively suppressing competing content while aligning the target with the readout. A causal intervention that suppresses interfering information improves performance, supporting the interference hypothesis. The study also finds that working memory performance in LLMs correlates with broader model capability, mirroring human findings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Working memory is the cognitive system that holds and manipulates information temporarily, and in humans it is limited to about three to four items. Large language models, despite having direct access to prior context via attention mechanisms, show similar limitations. The paper proposes that these limitations stem from representational compression and reuse, which support efficient learning but cause interference when multiple items are active simultaneously.

## [IACDM: An 8-Phase Framework to Bridge the AI Verification Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16399) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces IACDM, an 8-phase framework for AI-assisted software development that uses external verification agents and adversarial critique to address the verification gap. A pre-registered experiment over twelve projects found that all nineteen critique lenses are non-redundant, each discovering at least one unique defect. This framework directly tackles a critical issue in AI-assisted development: the verification gap, where developers may be slower and less accurate despite believing otherwise. By enforcing verification through an external state machine, IACDM could improve reliability and trust in AI-generated code, impacting software engineering practices and AI/ML integration. The framework's gate is enforced by a state machine outside the model, meaning the agent can request advancement but not grant it. The experiment also revealed two unplanned findings: the taxonomy withstands testing but the criterion for applying it does not, and the method creates commitments that are never re-confronted with the built system.

The paper builds on concepts like Perspective-Based Reading and adversarial critique, aiming to provide a refutable methodology for AI-assisted development.

## [Cat-DPO: Category-Adaptive Safety Alignment for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17299) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Cat-DPO introduces a novel direct preference optimization algorithm that assigns a separate adaptive safety margin to each harm category, dynamically adjusting margins based on the model's current performance per category. This approach replaces the traditional single-scalar safety margin with a per-category constrained optimization, improving both helpfulness and harmlessness across multiple backbones and baselines. This work addresses a critical limitation in LLM safety alignment: the uniform application of safety margins often leaves certain harm categories under-aligned. By offering a drop-in per-category refinement, Cat-DPO could significantly enhance the safety of deployed LLMs without sacrificing helpfulness, impacting both research and practical applications in AI safety. Cat-DPO is a single-stage training algorithm that achieves per-category dual updates at the same computational cost as standard DPO. It was evaluated across two LLM backbones and six preference-learning baselines, showing improved aggregate helpfulness and harmlessness, as well as reduced per-category safety variance and the best-to-worst gap.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is a 2023 alignment technique that bypasses explicit reward modeling and reinforcement learning by directly optimizing the policy against human preference pairs using a simple closed-form loss. Traditional safety alignment methods often collapse safety into a single scalar applied uniformly to all preference pairs, which can lead to uneven safety across harm categories. Cat-DPO builds on DPO by formulating safety alignment as a per-category constrained optimization problem, allowing adaptive safety margins that track each category's difficulty.

This paper introduces the Markov decision contest setting, proving that stationary Markov policies perform as well as history-dependent policies in long-horizon pairwise preference RL, and that optimal policy recovery is in P with a sublinear-convergence iterative algorithm. This work addresses a critical gap in RL with pairwise preferences, providing theoretical guarantees that bridge theory and practice. It could significantly improve the efficiency and reliability of AI alignment methods that rely on human pairwise feedback. The paper proves that the problem of recovering an optimal policy exactly is in P, and demonstrates a deep-learning variant of the iterative algorithm that works in long-term decision problems requiring function approximation. The setting is called the Markov decision contest.

**Background**: Reinforcement learning (RL) typically uses scalar rewards, but pairwise preferences are more natural for users and can express goals that scalar rewards cannot. However, methods for RL with pairwise preferences are inefficient in long-horizon problems and lack guarantees on Markov policies relative to history-dependent policies. A Markov decision process (MDP) is a framework with states, actions, transition rules, rewards, and a discount factor, and policies can be stationary, Markovian, or history-dependent.

## [COPSD: New Method to Prevent Collapse in Safety Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03089) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper identifies a collapse phenomenon in safety-oriented on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) and proposes a new method, Constitutional On-Policy Safety Distillation (COPSD), which includes a Cross-SFT cold-start and constitution-conditioned distillation. Experiments across 3 multimodal large language models on 12 benchmarks show that COPSD improves both safety and helpfulness while reducing the safety tax on general reasoning. This work addresses a critical issue in AI safety alignment, as OPSD is an efficient post-training paradigm but can collapse in safety tasks. The proposed COPSD method offers a practical solution to mitigate this collapse, potentially improving the reliability of safety alignment in large language models. The paper formalizes the collapse as 'geometric leakage' in a non-orthogonal semantic space, where safety pressure transfers into the expressiveness dimension. COPSD first calibrates the teacher through a Cross-SFT cold-start, then performs constitution-conditioned on-policy distillation, and experiments show improvements over baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) is a training strategy where a model uses its own outputs as supervision, often with a teacher conditioned on privileged information. Reverse KL divergence, which encourages mode-seeking behavior, is commonly used in such distillation but can lead to reduced expressiveness. The paper builds on prior work showing OPSD can collapse in verifiable reasoning tasks, and extends this to safety alignment, which is guided by high-level constitutions rather than explicit answers.

## [Study Shows Transformers Can Share QKV Projections with Minimal Loss](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04032) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A systematic study evaluated three QKV projection sharing constraints (Q-K=V, Q=K-V, Q=K=V) across synthetic, vision, and language modeling tasks, finding that sharing projections can match or slightly outperform standard transformers. Notably, Q-K=V sharing achieves 50% KV cache reduction with only 3.1% perplexity degradation in language modeling. This work provides a novel and practical approach to model compression and inference efficiency, directly benefiting edge deployment and on-device inference. The findings could influence future transformer designs, offering a complementary method to existing techniques like GQA and MQA for reducing memory footprint. The study shows that Q-K=V preserves quality because keys and values can occupy similar representational spaces and attention operates in a low-rank regime, whereas Q=K-V breaks attention directionality. Combining Q-K=V with GQA-4 yields 87.5% cache reduction, and with MQA achieves 96.9%, enabling practical on-device inference.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Transformers rely on query, key, and value (QKV) projections in their attention mechanism, which are typically separate weight matrices. KV cache is a memory optimization that stores key and value tensors during inference to avoid recomputation, but it grows with sequence length and model size, posing challenges for long contexts and edge devices. Techniques like GQA (Grouped Query Attention) and MQA (Multi-Query Attention) reduce KV cache by sharing heads, and this study explores sharing projections as an additional dimension.

## [SDS-LoRA: Fixing Anisotropic Gradient Scaling in Low-Rank Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16454) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces SDS-LoRA, a new low-rank parameterization that structurally decouples singular values from the backward pass, preventing anisotropic gradient scaling in LoRA. This method ensures gradients backpropagate only through orthonormal bases, improving convergence and reducing the gap to full fine-tuning. This work addresses a fundamental limitation in LoRA, a widely used parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique, potentially improving adaptation performance across many applications. By providing a theoretical analysis and a practical solution, it could influence future low-rank adaptation methods and benefit practitioners who rely on efficient fine-tuning. The paper shows that LoRA's gradient scaling is anisotropic, driven by singular values, which reduces the effective rank of gradients and misaligns them with the full fine-tuning gradient. SDS-LoRA's convergence rate is independent of the condition number of low-rank matrices, unlike LoRA, and experiments on natural language and vision benchmarks show improved loss convergence and adaptation performance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that updates pretrained models by adding low-rank matrices to weight updates, reducing trainable parameters. In deep learning, gradients are used to update model weights, and their scaling can affect training dynamics; anisotropic scaling means different directions are scaled differently, which can distort optimization. The paper proposes a geometric analysis of LoRA's gradient behavior and a solution to mitigate this issue.

## [Linguistic Diversity Slows Consensus in Neural Cellular Automata](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21202) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper introduces 'languages' as sub-populations in a Neural Cellular Automaton (NCA) that translate each other's messages with tunable 'linguistic distance', and shows that heterogeneity slows consensus and causes mild divergence rather than full fragmentation. The findings hold on both ring and 2D grid topologies and are interpreted as Ising relaxation with foreign-language regions acting as boundary defects. This work challenges the common assumption of a single communication protocol in collective intelligence models, offering a minimal mechanism—linguistic distance—that reproduces human-like group effects. It has implications for designing robust multi-agent systems and understanding consensus dynamics in complex systems. The NCA solves the density classification task, where cells must agree on a global majority without any individual observing it. A collective trained under diverse protocols is robust to mismatch, while a homogeneously trained one is not, and the results are qualitatively consistent with human group studies.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Neural Cellular Automata (NCAs) are computational systems where a grid of cells each run a learned local update rule, communicating only with neighbors. The density classification task (or majority problem) is a classic problem of finding local rules that perform majority voting. The Ising model is a mathematical model of ferromagnetism, and its relaxation dynamics are often used to study consensus and opinion dynamics in complex systems.

## [SkewAdam Cuts MoE Optimizer Memory by 97.4% via Tiered State Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19058) ⭐️ 8.0/10

SkewAdam, a new optimizer for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) training, reduces optimizer state memory from 50.6 GB to 1.29 GB (a 97.4% reduction) on a 6.78B-parameter model, cutting peak training memory from 81.4 GB to 31.3 GB, fitting within a 40 GB accelerator. It achieves this by allocating different optimizer state types to the backbone, experts, and router. This breakthrough enables large-scale MoE training on hardware with limited memory, potentially democratizing access to training large language models. It also challenges the conventional wisdom that uniform optimizer state is necessary, suggesting that where state is allocated matters as much as how much is used. SkewAdam uses float32 momentum plus a factored second moment for the backbone (5% of parameters), a factored second moment alone for the experts (95%), and an exact second moment for the router (<0.01%). In controlled tests over 82M tokens, it achieved validation perplexity 108.4, outperforming AdamW (126.8), Muon (120.2), and Lion (393.7), while maintaining router load balance within 1% of uniform.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models are large language models that use sparse activation, where only a subset of parameters (experts) are active per token, allowing for larger model capacity without proportional compute cost. However, training MoE models is memory-intensive, and the optimizer state (e.g., first and second moments in Adam) often dominates memory usage. Traditional optimizers like AdamW store full-precision moments for all parameters, which becomes prohibitive for very large models. Factored second-moment methods like Adafactor reduce memory by approximating the second moment with low-rank factors, but they may sacrifice accuracy. SkewAdam builds on these ideas by tailoring the state allocation to the distinct characteristics of each parameter population in an MoE.

The paper introduces the Dialogue Moral Hazard Game, a theory-grounded experimental paradigm based on Holmström's team moral-hazard model, to study hidden-action cooperation failures in multi-agent language models. ## [SE(3)-MeanFlow: Few-Step Protein Backbone Generation on Lie Groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27431) ⭐️ 8.0/10

SE(3)-MeanFlow extends the MeanFlow framework to the SE(3) Lie group geometry, enabling few-step protein backbone generation with simulation-free training. It introduces closed-form average-velocity identities for rotations and translations, along with an SE(3) alpha-Flow objective that removes the Jacobian-vector product from the rotation branch. This work addresses a significant computational bottleneck in diffusion-based protein design by reducing the number of network evaluations required during inference. It could accelerate high-throughput protein design campaigns and potentially improve the efficiency of generative models on Lie groups. SE(3)-MeanFlow matches or exceeds flow-matching baselines that use several times more sampling steps, and its advantage widens in the few-step regime. The method uses a warm-up stage with the alpha-Flow objective, followed by a small-t stabilized MeanFlow loss for pretraining and rectification-based post-training, at a modest cost in diversity.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Protein backbone generation often uses diffusion or flow-matching models on SE(3)^N, but inference requires numerically integrating an ODE over hundreds of network evaluations, each involving a Lie group exponential map. MeanFlow is a generative modeling framework that maps noise to data in one step by regressing interval-averaged velocity, and SE(3)-MeanFlow adapts this to the Lie group geometry of protein frames.

## [TCS-Bench: New Benchmark for LLM Theoretical CS Proofs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09538) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers introduced TCS-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating LLMs on research-level theoretical computer science (TCS) proof generation, using tasks from top venues like STOC, FOCS, and SODA. Their verification agent achieves over 90% accuracy on expert-labeled proof judgments. This benchmark fills a critical gap in evaluating LLMs for advanced mathematical reasoning, moving beyond simple problem-solving to research-level proof generation. It provides a standardized way to measure progress in AI-driven theorem proving, benefiting both the AI/ML and TCS communities. TCS-Bench includes theorem-proving tasks from papers at STOC, FOCS, and SODA, each providing necessary context for a self-contained proof. The verification agent was benchmarked against human expert judgments, achieving over 90% accuracy on the expert-labeled set.

## [Critic-Free Pretraining Boosts Offline-to-Online RL Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10473) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces Critic-Free Pretraining (CFP), a paradigm that eliminates offline critic training in offline-to-online reinforcement learning, allowing a freshly initialized critic to adapt without biased estimates. CFP is compatible with mainstream O2O algorithms and consistently matches or improves performance across diverse tasks. This work addresses a known problem in offline-to-online RL where offline-trained critics can misalign with the online environment, hindering fine-tuning. By proposing a simpler and more efficient approach, it could influence future O2O RL research and improve the practical deployment of RL agents. CFP is designed to be compatible with various mainstream O2O algorithms, and it shows particularly pronounced gains on several challenging tasks. The method avoids the need for offline critic training, which reduces computational overhead and eliminates the risk of biased value estimates.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Offline-to-online (O2O) reinforcement learning leverages policies pretrained on static datasets and then improves them through online interaction. In actor-critic methods, a critic estimates value functions to guide policy updates, but offline-trained critics may become misaligned when the policy and data distribution shift during online fine-tuning, leading to inefficient exploration and inaccurate policy improvement.

## [I/O-Aware Reformulation Boosts Wavelet Convolution Speed and Memory Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10805) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces I/O-aware algebraic reformulations and a fused implementation for wavelet convolutions (WTConv), reducing HBM traffic by approximately 2.55x and achieving up to 4.35x training speedup while halving peak memory usage. This work addresses a critical memory bottleneck in WTConv, a popular drop-in replacement for standard convolutions, making it more practical for real-world deep learning applications. The I/O-aware approach could inspire similar optimizations for other memory-bound neural network components. The reformulations include recomputing the Haar analysis butterfly on-chip, collapsing the multi-level synthesis cascade into a single closed-form pass, and folding learned per-channel scales into convolution weights. The evaluation uses the WTConvNeXt configuration across various decomposition levels and tensor shapes.

## [Verifiability Gap and Multilevel Governance for Agentic AI in FinTech](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11344) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces the Verifiability Gap concept and a multilevel governance theory for agentic AI in FinTech, backed by three empirical studies across nine model versions, from a 3-billion-parameter local model to a commercial frontier system. This work addresses a critical governance gap in FinTech, where agentic AI systems make consequential decisions with little oversight. The findings challenge the assumption that capability implies auditability, which could reshape regulatory and compliance practices in high-stakes financial domains. Study 1 shows that provider releases alter historical financial actions, and the frontier model rejects temperature, top_p, and top_k controls, exposing no random seed; under tightest controls, a local model reproduced 320/320 executions, hosted models 319/320 and 959/960. Study 2 reveals orchestration as a latent policy layer, with no execution record repeating across configurations; Study 3 shows deterministic credit models reproduce current actions perfectly but cannot recover historical ones, leading to evidence-contingent delegation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Agentic AI systems autonomously decompose goals, coordinate models and tools, and act with minimal human oversight, increasingly used in FinTech for consequential decisions. The Verifiability Gap refers to the shortfall between the verification required by delegated authority and the explainability and reproducibility retained after a decision, indexed to a verifier, evidentiary standard, and audit lag. Multilevel governance theory, originally from political science, describes the diffusion of authority across multiple levels, here applied to AI governance.

## [AI Guardrail Survival under Single-Cycle Agentic Self-Summarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11392) ⭐️ 8.0/10

This paper reveals that during single-cycle agentic self-summarization, safety rules can degrade into residues that fail to enforce behavior, highlighting the inadequacy of presence-based audits. This finding is critical for AI safety because it shows that merely checking for the textual presence of safety rules is insufficient; audits must verify that rules actually fire. It has significant implications for the evaluation and auditing of long-running AI agents, potentially influencing future safety mechanisms. The study found that degraded residues lead to prohibited actions far more often than intact rules, with gaps of +34 and +57 points under two replay models. It also documents evaluation pitfalls where LLM-judge labels alone would have reversed conclusions, and notes that textual loss is regime-dependent, with no observed textual severing mode.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Long-running agents periodically compact their context by replacing the transcript with a model-generated summary, a process known as context compaction. Previous work introduced 'Governance Decay' (Chen, 2026), showing that dropping a standing safety constraint during compaction drives behavioral violations. This paper builds on that by examining a single compaction cycle to understand how a safety rule is lost and what that implies for detection and evaluation.

## [RIFT: Rollout-Free Future Conditioning for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11521) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces RIFT (Rollout-free Imagination via Future Tokens), a method that allows world action models to generate actions without iterative video rollout by reusing a fixed future key/value cache. It achieves 98.8% success on LIBERO, close to rollout-based methods, while reducing action-chunk latency by 68.2% to 89.1%. This work significantly reduces deployment latency for world action models, making them more practical for real-time robot control. It challenges the assumption that iterative video rollout is necessary for action generation, potentially shifting the field toward more efficient future conditioning. The method separates cache consumption from production: models like Joint and Cosmos-2 can reuse a fixed final-clean K/V cache with minimal performance loss (97.9%-98.2% success), but still require iterative rollout to construct it. RIFT uses learned anticipation tokens to construct the complete future cache in one backbone pass, preserving the original future-read interface.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: World action models (WAMs) condition robot actions on predicted future states, often using iterative video rollout to generate those futures, which increases latency. The key/value (K/V) cache is a mechanism in transformer models that stores intermediate attention computations to speed up generation. LIBERO is a benchmark for lifelong robot learning, and RoboTwin is another evaluation environment for robotic manipulation.

## [Hamilton-Zero: Neural Tensor-Network Foundation Model for Quantum Ground States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11911) ⭐️ 8.0/10

The paper introduces Hamilton-Zero, a 0.5B parameter foundation model that learns ground states of arbitrary quadratic qubit Hamiltonians using manifold variational optimization and automatic differentiation. It is pre-trained on hundreds of thousands of Hamiltonian systems and fine-tuned on systems up to 1024 qubits, with evaluation on systems up to 8100 qubits. This work demonstrates a foundation model approach to quantum ground-state problems, potentially enabling amortized computation of ground states across many Hamiltonian systems, which could accelerate quantum chemistry and materials science simulations. It bridges machine learning and quantum many-body physics, offering a new tool for tackling problems beyond classical simulation reach. The model uses a manifold variational principle on SU(2)^N with centrally odd scalar functions, and the Hamiltonian acts via Lie derivatives computed by custom automatic differentiation primitives. Training employs an SU(2) replica-exchange Langevin sampler and sharded natural-gradient optimization with an extension of KFAC, on systems up to 64 qubits during pre-training.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Ground-state problems in quantum mechanics involve finding the lowest energy state of a Hamiltonian, which is crucial for understanding physical systems but often intractable for classical computers. Quadratic qubit Hamiltonians are a class of Hamiltonians expressed as polynomials of Pauli operators, common in quantum chemistry and condensed matter physics. Manifold variational optimization is a technique that performs optimization on a manifold, here used to represent quantum states as functions on SU(2)^N, avoiding explicit Hilbert-space vectors. Lie derivatives are a differential-geometric concept used to describe how functions change along vector fields, applied here to compute Hamiltonian actions.

## [Information Abundance Paradox: Long Contexts Hurt Parametric Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12218) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces the 'Information Abundance Paradox', showing that training with excessively long contexts can degrade parametric knowledge and performance, contrary to the assumption that more context is always better. This finding challenges the prevailing trend of scaling context windows in LLMs, suggesting that near-infinite context is not simply beneficial. It has significant implications for model training strategies and deployment, potentially reshaping how researchers approach long-context data. The paper finds that in pretraining, increasing context window improves performance only up to an intermediate optimum, after which it declines. Mechanistically, informative context shifts gradient pressure from feed-forward networks to attention modules, increasing reliance on context during inference.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) are trained with long contexts to capture richer evidence, assuming more context is always helpful. Parametric knowledge refers to information encoded in model weights, while contextualization relies on input context. This paper challenges the assumption by showing a trade-off between these two modes of learning.

This paper introduces Governed Persistent Memory (GPM), an auditable bitemporal state-transition model with source-bound admission and fail-closed release, designed to prevent agents from relying on contradictory or stale memory records. This work addresses a critical gap in long-horizon agent memory management by formalizing consistency and revocation, which is essential for reliable AI agents in production. The model includes five executable clauses covering ledger integrity, source binding, conflict isolation, non-revival after retraction, and exact claim closure. In the sealed V3 arm, the governed lane achieved 2,400/2,400 correct clusters versus 600/2,400 for ungoverned Qwen2.5-7B, with no regressions; a finite model explored 331,776 semantic and 1,990,656 query states without counterexamples.

Source-bound admission ties memory entries to their origin, preventing unverified information from being used in claims.

## [CAS: A Causal Attribution Score for Local and Global Explainable AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12555) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces the Causal Attribution Score (CAS), a framework that provides local and global causal explanations for machine learning models. It demonstrates improved accuracy over existing methods in simulations, with mean Local CAS MAE of 0.107 compared to 0.173 for one-at-a-time normalization. CAS addresses a key limitation in explainable AI by separating predictive attribution from causal effect attribution, which is crucial for decision-making in fields like policy and medicine. It provides a more accurate way to identify treatment-effect modifiers, potentially improving targeted interventions. The framework uses an interventional coalition game and causal Shapley contributions, and includes Local CAS, Signed Local CAS, and two Global CAS summaries. On empirical datasets, predictive SHAP rankings differed from Feature-CAS rankings, e.g., in Pennsylvania, 'dep1' moved from global rank 13 to Feature-CAS rank 2.

This is relevant for understanding treatment effect heterogeneity, which is important for personalized decision-making.

## [Reasoning Jury: Multi-Model Consensus Improves Reasoning Trace Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12585) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Reasoning Jury replaces a single LLM judge with a jury of open-weight models and a moderated consensus mechanism to identify reasoning defects in long reasoning traces. The system significantly outperforms frontier models like opus-4.6, sonnet-4.6, and gemini-3.1-pro in defect identification accuracy, while costing only 8-15% of the frontier model judge setup. This approach addresses a critical bottleneck in improving reasoning LLMs: the need for reliable, cost-effective judgment of long reasoning traces for data curation, reinforcement learning signals, and runtime feedback. By enabling open-weight models to outperform frontier models, it reduces dependency on expensive and often restricted frontier APIs, making high-quality reasoning evaluation more accessible. The moderated consensus mechanism involves a moderator who conducts deliberation among jurors, allowing them to critique each other's judgments and modify initial votes, then derives consensus through discussion or consolidation. The system also demonstrates how these judgments can be used to understand failure modes of reasoning LLMs on benchmarks, providing deeper performance insights.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Reasoning LLMs generate long chains of thought, and evaluating the quality of these reasoning traces is essential for improving model performance. Single-model judges, even frontier models, struggle to identify reasoning defects accurately, and using frontier models in online training is often prohibited by usage policies. Multi-agent consensus mechanisms, where multiple models deliberate and reach agreement, have been explored in various domains to improve decision quality.

## [Framework Measures and Restores Constraint Influence in Black-Box LLM Dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12599) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces a framework that measures and restores constraint influence in black-box LLM dialogues, addressing the problem of behavioral relapse when users revoke constraints. It includes a contract ledger, an ablation probe, and a repair ladder, all operating through the model API alone. This work addresses a novel and practical problem in multi-turn LLM dialogues: constraint revocation inertia. It provides measurable improvements in reducing relapse, which is crucial for LLM safety and reliability in real-world applications. The framework uses a contract ledger to pair constraints with executable checkers and record revocations as tombstones, an ablation probe to measure per-clause adherence, and a repair ladder operating under token- and attempt-matched budgets. Experiments show that ahead-of-time compilation significantly reduces relapse compared to a no-ledger baseline, while adaptive ladder interventions add no detectable gain.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: In multi-turn dialogues, users can revoke constraints, but models often continue to follow them, a phenomenon called behavioral relapse. This paper proposes a systematic framework to measure and repair this issue, using only the model API, without accessing internal weights.

This paper extends the incorporation of causal information from covariates and mediators into bounds for probabilities of causation (PoCs) from binary to multivalued settings, deriving tighter bounds than existing nonbinary bounds. The results are illustrated with toy examples and supported by simulation studies. This work addresses an open question in causal inference by showing that additional causal knowledge can tighten bounds for multivalued PoCs, which are crucial for individual-level causal assessment. It provides a theoretical foundation that could improve practical applications in fields like medicine and policy-making where multivalued treatments and outcomes are common. The paper builds on prior work by Tian and Pearl for binary PoCs and by Li and Pearl, as well as Shu et al., for multivalued settings. The proposed bounds incorporate causal information from covariates and mediators, and simulation studies demonstrate they are tighter than existing nonbinary bounds.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

This paper explores whether incorporating causal knowledge from covariates and mediators can further tighten these bounds.

## [SEAG: Privacy-Preserving RAG by Hiding Sensitive Entities from External LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12675) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces SEAG (Sensitive Entity Alias Generator), a framework that replaces sensitive entities in queries and retrieved documents with aliases before sending them to external LLMs, preventing data leakage. Experiments show all SEAG models achieve over 80% accuracy on the User metric, with Qwen-3, LLaMA-3.2, and Phi-4 achieving total hiding accuracies of 77.83%, 76.73%, and 74.91% respectively. This addresses a critical yet overlooked privacy gap in RAG systems, where external generators can access sensitive information in queries and retrieved documents. By enabling users to leverage powerful third-party LLMs without exposing confidential data, SEAG could enhance trust and adoption of RAG in privacy-sensitive domains like healthcare and finance. SEAG uses a lightweight model to locate sensitive entities, generate aliases, and construct an entity replacement table, which is applied to both the user's query and retrieved documents. The framework includes two newly constructed datasets: one for fine-tuning SEAG models and another for evaluating the entire framework, with experiments demonstrating its effectiveness.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) enhances LLM responses by retrieving relevant documents from external sources. While prior privacy research focused on preventing unauthorized access to sensitive data, the risk of external generators accessing confidential information in queries and retrieved documents has been largely overlooked. SEAG addresses this by anonymizing sensitive entities before they reach the external LLM, allowing users to benefit from third-party models without compromising privacy.

## [Spatial Memory Agent: Self-Evolving Frozen VLMs for Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12743) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Spatial Memory Agent (SMA), a runtime framework that enables frozen VLM agents to improve spatial reasoning through parameter-update-free self-evolution, without relying on external spatial tools at inference time. SMA converts verified spatial experience into reusable lessons and uses a Transfer Reliability Score (TRS) to guide retrieval during deployment. This work addresses an underexplored route in spatial intelligence: improving frozen VLM agents without weight updates or external tools, which could reduce computational costs and enable continuous improvement in embodied AI and robotic planning. The approach demonstrates strong performance across multiple benchmarks and base models, suggesting a practical path for spatial self-evolution. SMA uses verifier-guided reflection to distill compact transferable lessons from spatial experience, and assigns each lesson a Transfer Reliability Score (TRS) that is initialized uniformly and calibrated from retrieval outcomes. In read-only deployment, SMA retrieves lessons via semantic filter and similarity-TRS combined ranking. Across five spatial benchmarks and four base VLMs, SMA achieves the highest macro average in every base-model block and the best accuracy in most of the 20 evaluations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Spatial intelligence is crucial for embodied agents, robotic planning, and multimodal assistants. Existing approaches to improve spatial reasoning in VLM agents typically rely on post-training (e.g., supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning) or on calling external spatial tools (e.g., depth estimation, 3D reconstruction) during inference. SMA explores a complementary route: self-evolution of a frozen VLM through experience-grounded runtime memory, which avoids parameter updates and external tool dependency.

## [PROVE-RT: LLM-Assisted Mechanized Schedulability Proofs for Real-Time Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12762) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PROVE-RT is a new framework that uses large language models (LLMs) to automatically generate PROSA/ROCQ scripts for mechanized schedulability analysis. It achieves a 44.7% success rate on a curated evaluation set, whereas direct prompting of state-of-the-art LLMs fails to reliably produce valid mechanizations. This work addresses a significant bottleneck in formal verification of real-time systems by reducing the manual proof-engineering effort required to create mechanized proofs. It could make rigorous schedulability analysis more accessible to practitioners and advance the integration of LLMs into formal methods workflows. PROVE-RT uses dependency-aware informal sketches, retrieval from processed PROSA documentation, staged skeleton generation, and proof completion. The framework builds a mechanization-oriented corpus from 1,191 real-time systems papers, containing 13,134 informal sketches with dependency information.

Mechanized verification using tools like PROSA/ROCQ offers a rigorous alternative, yet manually constructing such proofs requires deep expertise. Large language models have shown promise in code generation, but they often lack domain-specific knowledge needed for formal verification. PROVE-RT addresses this by guiding LLMs with structured sketches and domain-specific retrieval.

## [ARAC-Bench: New Benchmark for Evaluating Auto-Research Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12788) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ARAC-Bench introduces a novel evaluation framework for Auto-Research systems, shifting the focus from final answers to the alignment and completeness of the research process. It comprises the Academic Cognition Skills system and a three-stage diagnostic protocol (Proposal, Experiment, Synthesis), and was validated on 11 state-of-the-art frameworks, with the best alignment score reaching only 67.9 out of 100. This benchmark addresses a fundamental gap in evaluating Auto-Research systems by measuring how well they mimic human research processes, not just outputs. It provides a fine-grained diagnostic tool and a scalable reward signal for training next-generation autonomous research systems, potentially accelerating progress in AI-driven scientific discovery. The framework's validation against Ph.D. candidates' rankings showed a strong correlation of 0.8141, confirming its reliability. The Academic Cognition Skills system is the first to transform implicit reviewer expertise into stage-calibrated, quantifiable rubrics, and the three-stage protocol decomposes the research process into traceable, mutually independent dimensions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Auto-Research systems are AI agents designed to autonomously conduct scientific research, but evaluating them has been challenging because traditional metrics focus on final answers rather than the quality of the research process. ARAC-Bench aims to mimic human research behavior by using rubrics derived from reviewer expertise and a modular diagnostic protocol. This approach is inspired by cognitive skills such as critical thinking and logical reasoning, which are essential for rigorous research.

## [CABS+ Improves Model Merging with Adaptive Weight Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12842) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CABS+ extends the prior CABS method by introducing Adaptive Weight Allocation (AWA), a gradient-free search scheme, and an asymmetric fitness function to reduce time complexity and improve multi-task performance. It also proposes Relative Synergy Score (RSS) to quantify model mergeability and guide model selection. This work addresses key limitations in model merging, a promising paradigm for building multi-task models without retraining. By improving efficiency and performance, CABS+ could accelerate adoption of model merging in real-world applications, especially where computational resources are limited. CABS+ was evaluated across 27 datasets and 5 models, including large language, small-scale language, and vision models. Compared with AdaMerging and WUDIMerging, it improves overall performance by 16.97% and 12.93%, respectively, uses less than 25% of AdaMerging's GPU memory, and achieves nearly a 4x speedup in merging time over WUDIMerging.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Model merging combines multiple task-specific models into a single multi-task model without additional training. However, parameter conflicts and knowledge interference across tasks can degrade performance. Prior work, CABS, used structured pruning and sequential masking to reduce interference, but relied on grid search for scaling coefficients, leading to exponential time complexity and suboptimal optimization.

## [India's Consumer Protection Act 2019 and AI Liability Gaps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12863) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new working paper on arXiv examines whether India's Consumer Protection Act, 2019, can adequately address harms from AI products and services, identifying gaps in liability allocation and causation. The paper argues that while the Act's definitions are technology-agnostic, proving causation and mapping the AI value chain to existing legal categories remain significant challenges. This analysis is significant because India is a major market for AI products, and clear liability rules are essential for consumer protection and innovation. The paper highlights legal gaps that could affect how AI-related harms are litigated, influencing policy and industry practices in India and potentially other jurisdictions. The paper notes that AI failures often stem from design choices rather than discrete defects, complicating causation. It also points out that the AI value chain involves overlapping responsibilities among data providers, model developers, deployers, and users, which do not neatly fit the Act's categories of manufacturers, sellers, and service providers.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: The Consumer Protection Act, 2019, is India's primary legislation for addressing consumer grievances, including product liability. As AI systems become more prevalent in consumer applications, questions arise about how existing laws apply to AI-related harms, especially given the opaque and distributed nature of AI decision-making. Similar debates are occurring globally, such as in the EU and Canada, where proving causation in AI product liability cases is also difficult.

## [ReflectFact: Self-Reflective Agents for Multi-Hop Fact Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12877) ⭐️ 7.0/10

ReflectFact introduces a novel self-reflective agent framework for multi-hop fact verification, incorporating three key tasks: Explicit Reasoning Path Planning, Evidence-Drift Verification, and Reasoning Reflection Verification. It achieves state-of-the-art performance on HOVER and EX-FEVER, outperforming the strongest baselines by 3.32% and 2.78% respectively. This work addresses critical limitations in existing multi-agent fact verification methods, such as insufficient global objective awareness and conflicts between parametric knowledge and evidence. By improving comprehension and reasoning, ReflectFact has the potential to enhance misinformation detection systems, which is increasingly important in the era of social media. ReflectFact's Explicit Reasoning Path Planning resolves implicit entities, decomposes claims into sub-questions, and integrates verified facts into a verdict. Evidence-Drift Verification forces the agent to re-answer by quoting supporting evidence when a grounded answer merely echoes parametric prior, while Reasoning Reflection Verification re-examines each reasoning step and regenerates it upon detecting inconsistencies like location bias and replacement bias.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-hop fact verification is the task of verifying claims by reasoning over multiple pieces of evidence, which is crucial for combating misinformation on social media. Traditional single-hop verification methods struggle with claims requiring connections across multiple sources. Recent approaches use multi-agent collaboration to decompose the task into specialized subtasks, but they often suffer from agents deviating from the global objective and conflicts between parametric knowledge and evidence. Self-reflective agent frameworks, which incorporate explicit self-critique steps, have been explored to improve reasoning and decision-making in LLM-based agents.

This paper introduces Predictive Memory Localization (PML), a method that predicts selective intervention paths from internal signals, and demonstrates improved localization over random baselines across diverse datasets. The frozen study covers 3,000 records from nine datasets and fourteen domains, yielding 30,000 distinct record-direction-layer paths and 210,000 distinct path-strength evaluations. This work advances activation steering and mechanistic interpretability by providing a falsifiable framework for predicting selective intervention outcomes, which could improve the reliability and safety of model editing and control. It offers a risk-aware decision policy that reduces semantic-neighbor damage, potentially benefiting applications in model alignment and knowledge editing. At layer 7, the geometry-derived RFM/AGOP direction reaches 13.1% target-any and 12.3% clean-any, exceeding random by 3.6 and 3.4 percentage points under a record-paired bootstrap. Across three residual-norm-matched base models, learned directions retain selective-path gains and low-dose responses yield 0.801-0.828 record-held-out macro AUROC.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Activation steering modifies a language model's hidden states at inference time to control its behavior, but localization alone does not reveal whether a direction has a selective operating regime. PML treats the measured-grid intervention path as the predictive object of memory localization, separating random-calibrated target movement from semantic-neighbor and capability damage. The method compares static localization and supervised geometry with a strength-disjoint low-dose causal response, aiming to predict margin-level selective outcomes.

BoardroomAI introduces a dependency-aware, human-steerable multi-agent deliberation framework that uses evolving decision graphs to allow persistent human intervention. The framework includes an intervention compiler, dependency-aware propagation, and an evaluation framework, demonstrating efficient selective repair in synthetic tests. This framework addresses a critical gap in multi-agent systems by enabling humans to intervene throughout the decision process, not just at the start. It could improve collaborative decision-making in organizations by allowing dynamic adjustments to evidence, constraints, and priorities, potentially leading to more valid and context-aware decisions. In experiments with 600 generated decision-DAG interventions, the propagation method matched exhaustive impact computation while inspecting only 14.59% of nodes. In a 12-case pilot, selective repair recomputed 62.11% of canonical nodes, preserved all gold-unaffected nodes, and produced valid decisions in six cases, abstaining in the other six due to insufficient context.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Traditional transcript-based multi-agent systems typically take an initial problem, have agents deliberate internally, and return a final response, with limited human involvement. BoardroomAI treats humans as persistent participants who can challenge assumptions, modify constraints, and redirect the process, using a typed decision graph to represent dependencies and an intervention compiler to translate human actions into graph updates.

## [DMDIntel: Interpreting LLMs via Dynamic Mode Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13048) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DMDIntel is a new method that applies dynamic mode decomposition (DMD) to interpret LLM predictions in classification tasks, attributing importance to input tokens by decomposing hidden states into modes and ranking tokens based on projection values. Experiments across three datasets and three model families show it outperforms state-of-the-art techniques like PCA, integrated gradients, and SHAP. This work introduces a novel interpretability approach that could provide more faithful and effective explanations for LLM predictions, addressing a critical need for transparency in AI systems. By outperforming existing attribution methods, DMDIntel may become a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners seeking to understand and trust LLM behavior. The method first decomposes hidden states into prominent modes using DMD, then ranks input tokens based on projection values onto those modes. The paper reports consistent improvements over baseline methods across multiple datasets and model families, but does not provide details on computational overhead or limitations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) are often used as black boxes, making it difficult to understand why they make certain predictions. Input attribution methods aim to explain predictions by assigning importance scores to input tokens, helping users identify which parts of the input influenced the model's output. Dynamic mode decomposition (DMD) is a mathematical technique from fluid dynamics that extracts dominant spatiotemporal patterns from complex systems, which DMDIntel adapts for analyzing LLM hidden states.

## [VALG: Agentic System Automates ML Theory Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13060) ⭐️ 7.0/10

VALG is an agentic system that automates machine learning theory research by combining multi-level verification, adaptive problem formulation, and graph-structured proof development. It was evaluated on nine subproblems from five COLT 2026 open problems, with two runs producing theorem candidates matching the scope of their source briefs. This work demonstrates a novel application of AI to automate theorem proving and problem formulation in machine learning theory, potentially accelerating research in this niche field. It could inspire further development of agentic systems for other areas of theoretical computer science and mathematics. VALG maintains a fixed mathematical specification within each source-relative theorem branch, checks theorem-level composition of a typed proof-dependency graph, and constructs and reviews local proofs in dependency order. When proof attempts fail, it identifies whether the obstruction lies in a derivation, proof structure, or theorem formulation, and routes the next attempt accordingly, with formulation-level obstructions initiating explicitly related variants or relaxations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Machine learning theory studies learning procedures through mathematical setups where data models, training protocols, oracle access, loss, metrics, and randomness define the phenomena that theorems explain. Solving open problems requires developing problem formulation, theorem targets, and proof mechanisms in concert, a process that VALG aims to automate. The system is open source and available on GitHub.

This paper empirically reprograms open-weight LLMs into proactive Socratic assistants, defining an optimal LoRA rank threshold at r=16 and a training window of e∈[2,3] under HPC constraints, with a minimum validation loss of 0.919. Scaling to 14B parameters achieved a lower perplexity of 1.414, and DPO decoupled assertive behavior from syntax. This work challenges the default passive assistant paradigm, offering concrete bounds for PEFT and LoRA rank that enable compute-efficient behavioral modification. It provides a rigorous empirical framework for cross-lingual persona transfer, which is valuable for alignment research and HPC-constrained environments. Cross-lingual stress testing revealed robust alignment in closely related language families but degradation in morphologically distant targets.

This paper proposes a unified evidence reasoning framework that integrates chaos-conflict measurement and historical-experience weighting to improve Dempster-Shafer fusion under conflicting evidence. The framework introduces five formally proven properties for the chaos-conflict measure and applies spectral clustering and regret theory for context-specific reliability profiling. This work addresses two persistent challenges in Dempster-Shafer theory: the independent assessment of inter-evidence conflict and intra-evidence uncertainty, and the lack of long-term reliability exploitation in fusion methods. By achieving state-of-the-art performance on 16 benchmark datasets, it offers a robust solution for multi-source decision making, potentially impacting fields like sensor fusion, pattern recognition, and AI reasoning. The framework uses a hybrid combination rule that adaptively balances uncertainty preservation against weighted consensus, controlled by the global conflict level. Experiments show an average F1 score of 85.78 and mean AUC of 93.30, outperforming eight DST-based baselines and three gradient boosting methods, with ablation analysis confirming each component's contribution.

## [SkillEvo: Multi-Turn Feedback for Self-Renewing Agent Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13120) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SkillEvo introduces a method that uses multi-turn interaction feedback to generate self-renewing evolution gradients for improving AI agent skills. It surpasses self-reflection-based evolution by 23.0 points and single-turn-QA-driven evolution by 15.4 points across six cloud service categories, 9 production skills, and 98 skill-reference files. This addresses a key limitation in agent skill evolution: single-turn evaluation feedback decays after initial patches, causing evolution to stall. By providing continuous, trustworthy gradients from multi-turn interactions, SkillEvo enables sustained improvement, which is crucial for real-world AI agents that encounter complex, multi-step failures. SkillEvo consists of two components: a feedback generator that uses multi-turn user simulation to expose defects layer by layer, and a governance layer that actively repairs factual degradation and structural bloat instead of passively rejecting candidates. The method was evaluated on cloud services, production skills, and skill-reference files, showing significant improvements over baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Agent skills are reusable, pre-packaged instructions and workflows that let an AI agent handle tasks without relearning from scratch. Traditionally, they are either hand-authored or generated in a single LLM pass, lacking a closed loop for improvement from interaction failures. Recent work closes this loop but relies on single-turn question-answering evaluation, which fails to capture defects that surface only across multiple turns. SkillEvo addresses this by using multi-turn feedback to sustain evolution gradients.

A new arXiv paper demonstrates that post-normalization outperforms pre-normalization in Transformers when depth is introduced via curriculum growing, contrary to the standard preference for pre-norm. In a controlled distillation study with a Qwen3-8B teacher and a nine-layer student, post-norm improved validation cross-entropy by 0.0328 over pre-norm under curriculum growth, while they were nearly identical under joint training. This finding challenges a common assumption in LLM training and suggests that normalization placement and training curriculum should be considered coupled design choices. It could influence future training strategies for large language models, potentially improving efficiency and performance in curriculum-based depth growing approaches. The study includes a post-joint control matched by student active-layer tokens, which rules out compute as the sole explanation for the improvement. Diagnostics show that post-norm maintains stable residual scales, while pre-norm exhibits structural-token scale drift, and the final pre-grow block is nearly identity-mapped on a fixed batch.

TsuGO is a new process-level reasoning benchmark that uses Go life-and-death problems to evaluate search efficiency in LLM reasoning. It parses chain-of-thought into a structured search tree and reports Search Efficiency alongside Token Efficiency and other diagnostic metrics. This benchmark addresses a gap in LLM reasoning evaluation by focusing on how models organize search and allocate reasoning resources, rather than just final-answer accuracy. It provides a structured, verifiable testbed that could drive improvements in model reasoning strategies and resource efficiency. TsuGO constrains the solution space to disentangle domain knowledge from search organization, and experiments show that current LLMs remain far from stable tsumego solving. Stronger models succeed by finding correct candidates earlier and sustaining effort on productive branches, but most behave closer to unguided search algorithms than to neural-guided KataGo.

This paper introduces a sheaf-theoretic model for diagnosing and repairing failures in agent harnesses, validated through a controlled experiment and a real-repository stress test on SWE-bench Multilingual data. This work addresses a critical challenge in AI agent reliability by providing a formal framework for compositional repair, potentially improving the design and debugging of complex agent systems. In the real-repository test, a candidate-indexed repair resolves 118 issues versus 116 for a matched noncohomological selector, but the difference is not statistically significant (sign-flip p=0.75), and the leave-one-repository-out abstention gate ties the strong anchor at 127/160, so the discovery gate fails.

## [ParliamentRAG: Authority-Aware RAG for Italian Parliamentary Proceedings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13410) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced ParliamentRAG, a RAG system for the Italian Chamber of Deputies that incorporates a topic-dependent authority model to mitigate speaker dominance, weight topical expertise, and reduce citation misattribution. In evaluations against Google NotebookLM on 15 policy topics, it achieved higher coverage across political groups (0.97 vs. 0.95), perfect quotation faithfulness (1.00 vs. 0.95), and stronger expert preferences on source-related dimensions. This work addresses critical risks in applying RAG to parliamentary transcripts, which are essential for democratic transparency. By improving coverage and citation accuracy, it can help citizens, journalists, and researchers access balanced and trustworthy information from legislative records, potentially influencing how AI systems handle politically sensitive text. The authority model combines interpretable components such as profession, education, and previous interventions, and is query-dependent. The system retrieves relevant speech chunks, identifies topic-relevant experts across parliamentary groups, and generates summaries with supporting quotations. Evaluation used a two-level protocol with automated metrics and blind A/B human evaluation by six domain experts.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that enhances large language models by retrieving relevant information from external knowledge bases before generating responses. Parliamentary proceedings are voluminous and fragmented, making multi-perspective access difficult. Applying RAG to such transcripts introduces risks like dominance of frequent speakers, inability to weight expertise, and citation misattribution, where generated claims are not properly supported by retrieved sources.

## [RAIL: Unified AI Readiness Level Classifier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13428) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper introduces the Unified AI Readiness Level (AIRL), a nine-level ordinal scale that integrates existing AI readiness frameworks, and proposes RAIL, a panel-of-experts classifier using large language models to automatically assess AI technology maturity from natural-language descriptions. This work addresses a practical gap in evaluating AI maturity, providing a unified and automatic assessment method that could benefit investment decisions, project management, and policy monitoring. It also reduces the risk of overestimation compared to monolithic LLM classifiers, enhancing reliability in AI governance. The AIRL scale is built on an environmental evidence ladder and includes dimensional caps covering specification, data existence, data quality, data legality, expert knowledge, and algorithmic maturity, along with a generality-anchoring rule. RAIL consists of one evidence agent and six independent dimension agents, with a deterministic minimum rule aggregating verdicts and a chief expert who can only lower the panel's recommendation, never raise it above the caps.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Technology Readiness Levels (TRLs) are a method for estimating the maturity of technologies during the acquisition phase of a program, enabling consistent discussions of technical maturity. However, adapting TRLs to AI lacks AI-specific gating criteria, and other frameworks like Machine Learning Technology Readiness Levels (MLTRL) presuppose access to internal process artifacts, while AI/data readiness dimension models use scales that resist direct comparison. This paper unifies these frameworks into a single scale that can be automatically assessed from natural-language descriptions.

MARC v1 is an open-source framework that replaces monolithic LLM prompting with deterministic multi-agent orchestration for clinical reasoning, featuring role-specialized agents and a Decomposer module for automatic prompt generation. It is configurable via YAML and supports both API-based and local CPU-compatible deployments. MARC coordinates agents for extraction, reasoning, answer generation, and evaluation, with explicit context passing and traceable intermediate outputs. The Decomposer module generates task-specific prompts from plain-language descriptions, and the framework is model-agnostic and entirely YAML-configurable without code changes.

## [QuoteBench Reveals Matched Scores Hide Command-Path Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13547) ⭐️ 7.0/10

QuoteBench, a new benchmark, measures how execution transport interfaces can hide command-path failures in LLM coding agents. It uses 56 one-shot tasks from 14 incident-derived families and shows that matched execution scores alone are insufficient. This matters because it reveals a critical gap in evaluating LLM coding agents: matched scores can mask significant performance differences caused by execution transport issues. It urges the community to report configuration details and final-state validators, potentially changing how agents are benchmarked and compared. The benchmark crosses the generation contract with an added parser that deliberately leaves one escape unescaped. Across eight configurations, replaying the same reply through the added parser lowers success by 55.4 to 73.2 percentage points; disclosure recovers 30.4 to 60.7 points for six configurations, with zero or negative recovery for the other two. GPT-5.6-sol's matched gap of -3.6 points hides -64.3 points of damage and +60.7 points of compensation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: LLM coding agents issue Bash commands through interfaces that may serialize, wrap, and reparse model output. Matched execution scores, which compare the final state to expected outcomes, cannot distinguish errors in command generation from failures introduced during execution transport. QuoteBench addresses this by using exact final-state validation and a deliberately unescaped parser to isolate the boundary between generation and transport.

A new controlled benchmark fine-tunes six multilingual pre-trained models on the OpenSLR SLR54 Nepali corpus (~165 hours) under identical protocols. Whisper-Large-v3-Turbo (14.76% WER) and IndicWav2Vec (14.89% WER) achieve statistically similar performance despite a 9x parameter gap and 40x pretraining-data gap. This provides the first standardized, efficiency-aware benchmark for Nepali ASR, offering empirical evidence that language-family proximity can substitute for raw model scale. The findings could guide model selection for low-resource languages, favoring smaller, faster CTC models over larger autoregressive ones when latency matters. The benchmark evaluates Word Error Rate (WER), Character Error Rate (CER), and Real-Time Factor (RTF) on three independent test sets: OpenSLR, FLEURS, and Common Voice. CTC decoders run up to 29x faster than autoregressive Whisper at the same accuracy, and MMS-1B shows the smallest out-of-domain degradation on FLEURS (+12.55 pp), indicating scale buys robustness rather than peak in-domain accuracy.

AnchorSIPS introduces a synthetic dataset of 10,000 structured psychosis-risk interviews, each modeled on the Mini-SIPS interview, with transcript-grounded measurement targets. The dataset is generated via a plan-then-realize pipeline that fixes labels and structure before LLM generation to avoid inter-turn inconsistencies. This dataset addresses the data-access bottleneck in AI for psychosis-risk assessment, enabling research without privacy constraints of real clinical interviews. It provides a benchmark for evidence extraction and transcript-grounded measurement, potentially advancing clinical NLP and mental health AI. The dataset includes 24 symptom questions, follow-up evidence, decisions on delusion-like, hallucination-like, and disorganized communication symptoms, exclusion of frank psychosis, and a final APS diagnosis. Each intermediate decision is anchored to supporting transcript turns, and the APS diagnosis depends on earlier endorsements and checks.

## [StorySpark: Evolutionary Search Framework for Story Premise Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12336) ⭐️ 7.0/10

StorySpark introduces a module-wise evolutionary search framework for story premise generation, treating narrative modules as local search spaces with feedback-driven mutation and Pareto-guided selection. It produces stronger final premises than competitive baselines, with consistent gains in originality. This addresses an underexplored area in LLM-based story generation—premise ideation—and offers an interpretable, evolutionary approach that could enhance creative AI research. It also demonstrates that better premises lead to higher-quality downstream stories, which is valuable for narrative generation systems. The framework operates over interpretable narrative modules such as background, persona, event, ending, and twist, and uses Pareto-guided selection to balance branch coverage with promising directions. Multi-view automatic and human evaluations show consistent gains in originality, and expanded stories maintain completeness, fascination, and diverse narrative directions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Story premise generation is the initial creative step in storytelling, but most LLM-based research has focused on later stages like planning and coherence. Evolutionary search algorithms, inspired by natural selection, iteratively improve solutions through mutation and selection, and Pareto-guided selection handles multiple objectives by preserving non-dominated solutions.

## [LLMs Mimic Biases Without Understanding Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12339) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new preprint (arXiv:2608.12339) shows that large language models like ChatGPT-4o and Qwen exhibit social proof bias and loss aversion even when human behaviors are not indicative of actual preferences, and even when biases are explicitly described as such. The study identifies two mechanisms: faulty mimicry of preferences and mimicry of explicitly biased behaviors. This research is significant for AI alignment and behavioral economics, as it reveals that LLMs can inherit biases not from biased data but from faulty inference processes. It suggests that scientific reports on biases can become self-fulfilling prophecies in LLM responses, which has implications for how we interpret and mitigate model biases. The study conducted four experiments focusing on economic biases, using ChatGPT-4o and Qwen. They found that when prompted with reports of human behaviors that were clearly non-indicative of preferences, the models still showed social proof bias; and when loss aversion was explicitly described as a bias, the models displayed it, with the extent of bias in scientific reports predicting the models' own bias.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models (LLMs) are trained on vast amounts of human-generated text, which can contain various social, affective, and cognitive biases. Previous research has shown that LLMs often mirror these biases, but the underlying mechanisms are not fully understood. This study distinguishes between biases arising from biased training data and those arising from faulty mimicry of human behavior, even when the behavior is not logically related to preferences. The concept of loss aversion comes from prospect theory in behavioral economics, where losses are perceived as more impactful than equivalent gains.

## [GraphGym: A Unified Multi-Dimensional Benchmark for Complex Graph Reasoning in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12391) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces GraphGym, a five-stage semi-automatic framework for constructing complex graph reasoning benchmarks, and presents a benchmark with 202 tasks. It expands coverage along five dimensions: graph size, task complexity, task description, graph loading, and task source, and evaluates LLMs under text-based, code-based, and augmented reasoning settings. This work addresses limitations in existing graph reasoning benchmarks, which have limited data complexity coverage and rely heavily on manual construction. By providing a unified and diagnostic benchmark, it enables more comprehensive evaluation of LLM reasoning abilities and offers empirical guidance for future enhancement methods, potentially influencing the development of more robust LLMs. The framework uses an LLM-based data generator to automatically produce task descriptions, graph data, reference solutions, graph-loading scripts, question forms, and evaluation scripts, while retaining human validation at key quality-control stages. Experiments show that complexity dimensions reveal model limitations less visible in existing benchmarks; fine-tuned models struggle to generalize to GraphGym, while retrieval-augmented methods show scenario-dependent adaptability, improving textual reasoning but not consistently improving coding reasoning.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Aug 15, 04:00

**Background**: Graph reasoning is a promising testbed for evaluating LLM reasoning abilities because graph instances can be programmatically generated, structurally controlled, and naturally scaled to long-input settings. Existing benchmarks often lack coverage of data complexity and rely on manual construction, and they do not unify evaluation across text-based and code-based reasoning modes. GraphGym aims to fill this gap by providing a semi-automatic framework that expands benchmark coverage along multiple dimensions.
